The bug is the jewel beetle, what the big jungle fish love (arowana leap for beetles), for part three's drone fishing. Five of the course's rings are big orange flowers instead (rings 4, 11, 18, 25 and 32, so 30 rings are left), each hanging on a vine from the canopy with a pink ring in its middle (entities/droneflight.ts TRAP_EVERY, render/droneflight.ts drawTrapFlower). The drone carries three traps (DRONE_TRAPS in data/drone.ts), shown under the ring count as a jar and a number. Fly through a flower's middle with a trap aboard and a little glass jar is hung there ("Trap hung! By tomorrow it will be full of jewel beetles."); from the next day on it glows green with beetles crawling in it, and flying through again brings 3 beetles home (BEETLES_PER_TRAP, up to 99) and the trap back aboard. A trap still filling is left be ("Come back tomorrow!"), and a flower with no trap aboard to hang says so. Caterman's card says when traps are full and counts beetles and traps (aboard, hung, full). The flight's summary adds the beetles brought home. Trap capacity is for part four's upgrades.
- Fishing from the drone (2026-10-22), part three of the owner's drone ("to then fish via the drone"), as the plan put to her (hers to change): island 11's black lagoon, inland where no boat can go, fished with a jewel beetle on the drone's line (entities/dronefish.ts, render/droneflight.ts drawDroneLagoon). Caterman's card has Fish the lagoon (N beetles) once the drone is yours (needing a beetle and room in the hold). Seen from behind the drone as the jungle course is, it flies on slowly by itself (6 a second) down 220 of black water between reedy banks under the jungle, the thumb (or the keys) steering it across. Fish rise ahead in spreading rings, one every 14 to 22 from 24 on, the same every pass (seed 1171): 3 in 10 arowanas, the rest archerfish. Drop the beetle (the button, or space) with the drone over a rise, within 2 across and from 2 past to 3.5 short of it, and it hovers while the line goes down (0.7 s); the fish strikes (0.9 s: an arowana leaps right out of the water for it with a splash, an archerfish spits a jet up at it), then Reel it in! for 1.8 s, a gold ring closing round the fish hanging off the line. Reeled in, it is caught (into the hold and the log, a first one into the field guide) and the beetle is gone; too slow, the fish lets go and the beetle is still on the line; dropped on empty water, it is wound back up ("Nothing there"). Two new species: 39 arowana (120, long and gold with red fins) and 40 archerfish (30, silver with black bands), in the field guide (data/guide.ts LAGOON_SPS). Land the drone ends a pass early; the pass ends at the lagoon's far end, and what it caught is said.
- The drone's upgrades (2026-10-22), part four of the owner's drone ("make the drones custom color upgradable"): an Upgrades shelf on Caterman's card under the Fly and Fish buttons, four of them, each two levels up from what the drone comes with, 1,500 for the first level and 3,000 for the second (data/drone.ts DRONE_UPGRADES, UPGRADE_PRICE; stars by the name for each level, Best when done). A bigger battery: 100, then 130, then 160 charge (BATTERY_LV; the bar fills to it). Faster rotors: how quickly it answers the thumb and how fast across and up, 6/7/5, then 8/8.5/6, then 10/10/7 (HANDLING_LV, DroneFlight.setHandling). Another trap: 3, then 4, then 5 aboard, one for every flower (TRAPS_LV). A longer line: a dropped beetle reaches a rise 2, then 2.6, then 3.2 across (LINE_LV, DroneFishing.reach). All four for 18,000.
- The mermaids, part one (2026-10-22), the owner's ("Let's start mermaids", after the roadmap's sirens and mermaids), a surprise for the kid, her answers: the verb is singing their tune back, the pearls make the siren charm, the rocks are off island 2, and the sirens are his to design once he has found the mermaids. Seven rocks in an arc in the shallows off island 2's far shore, 375 out from its middle at 80 degrees round, between two of its parrotfish schools (world/mermaids.ts ROCKS, MER_ROCKS; render/mermaids.ts drawMerRock, two tiers of grey stone with foam round each, shells and a starfish on the three big ones, weed on the small; sorted with the boat, which is pushed off each as off an island). From the start of dusk until midnight (merHours; MIDNIGHT is halfway through the night, the sirens' hours to come) three sisters sit one to each big rock (entities/mermaids.ts MER_LOOKS): Marina, gold hair and a teal tail; Ondine, red hair and a green tail; Pearl, white hair and a lilac tail. At dusk they swim in one after another (STAGGER 1.3 s) and climb up (RISE_T 1.6), and at midnight they slip back in. Each sits facing the boat, her tail over the front of her rock and down it, its fin flicking at the water, a glint running down it now and then; at night each shines a little, a light in the night mask and a glow in her tail's colour, so they can be found. Within 900 (MER_EARSHOT) they sing, a phrase of four to six notes every four to eight seconds from each in turn (merPhrase: stepping among four notes, the last held), the singer's arm out and her mouth open, each note drifting up off her in its own colour (NOTE_COLOURS, the shells' colours to come). Their notes are F4, G4, A4 and C5 (audio/ambience.ts MER_NOTES, mermaidNote: a sine and a soft octave with a slow vibrato, into the whales' echo, heard from the figure's side), and near them their own chord takes over the pad (MER_CHORD, an F major seventh, past MER_TAKEOVER of their presence). A boat faster than 200 within 280 sends them under in a flick of tails and a splash (SPOOK, SPOOK_NEAR); they come back up seven seconds after it has slowed below 110 (HIDE_T, GENTLE). Heard first: a toast, and a note at the screen's edge pointing to them until they are found; within 320 (MER_SIGHT), seen: a toast, a gold Mermaids found chip in the log (before, once island 2 is found, Singing at dusk?), a page in the guide with where and when, and Mermaid rocks on the sea map. A boat racing in sees them and frightens them in the same moment, so a fright within five seconds of the first sighting says both in one message, or the second would be lost behind the first. Seen from the figure ashore as well as from the boat, and a figure ashore never frightens them. By day the rocks are bare, and the first visit says to come back at dusk. Not heard or seen from inside a tower or a dive. Next: their song sung back on four shells, for a pearl (part two, below).
- Singing with the mermaids, part two (2026-10-22), the owner's verb for them ("sing it back"), on the plan put to her (one pearl a night, the tunes growing, the paint on the first; hers to change). Stopped by their rocks in their hours (within 300 and slower than 50, aboard, they up and not hiding), a Sing with the mermaids button; pressed, a card of four shells comes up over the bottom of the screen, pink, gold, blue and lilac, low to high, the colours of their notes (index.html #shells). One of the sisters, each night the next in turn, sings a tune on their four notes (entities/mersong.ts merTune: stepping among them by one or two, never the same note twice running), the shells lighting as she sings, and then it is yours: tap the shells in the same order (or keys 1 to 4), each ringing a bell on its note (audio/ambience.ts shellNote). A wrong note shakes its shell, Not quite, and she sings it again, a quarter slower each time down to 1.6 times as slow (SLOWER, SLOWEST): nothing is lost. Sung right, the three of them sing together, a chord, and she gives you a pearl: one a night (`mer.night`, the day of the last), and Tonight's pearl is yours if you stop by again. The tunes start at three notes and grow a note with each pearl given, to seven (tuneLength). The first pearl brings their paint, Mermaid scales (data/tuning.ts PAINTS, the eleventh, MER_PAINT: a sea-glass teal hull, lilac trim, violet roof, pink flag, and glints running along the hull's sides), which no tier unlocks (TIER_PAINTS 10), its swatch in the shop a shimmer of its colours, hidden until then. Each pearl's message counts toward the five that make a charm against the sirens (part three). Sailing off (faster than 110 or 380 away), or them going under or to bed, puts the song by for now. While it is up, their own phrases hush (Mermaids.hush) and the radar stands aside. The log's chip becomes Mermaid pearls N, and the guide's page says how to earn them.
- The siren charm, the mermaids' part three (2026-10-22), the owner's ("Let's do the charm now too", before the sirens; her pick from the first round, the pearls make the charm). The pearl that makes five (CHARM_PEARLS, entities/mersong.ts): the three of them sing a high note together and string the five into a charm, hung on your boat; five pearls held come off, the count of those given stays (`mer.charm` in the save; a save already holding five has it strung the next time it stops by them). It hangs across the cabin's front on a pale string, five pearls with a glint each and a pink shell in the middle, hidden behind the cabin when the bow is turned away (drawShip's charm). After midnight the sirens' song cannot pull a boat that wears it: the sirens are next, Greek ones, as the kid asked. Pearls keep coming one a night after it, for the collection (Mermaid pearls N), and the tunes keep growing. A Siren charm chip in the shop's Yours row and in the log, and a line on the guide's page.
- The sirens (2026-10-22), the kid's ("do the sirens like greek mythology!") with the owner's calls: as the oldest Greek pictures have them, great dark birds with women's heads; their song steers a boat onto the rocks, which knock the hull; the pearl charm the only answer besides sailing hard. A little after midnight (SIREN_FROM, world/mermaids.ts), once the mermaids have slipped away, three of them fly in out of the dark one after another (COME_T 2.4 s, SIREN_STAGGER 0.9) and perch on the mermaids' three big rocks until dawn, when they fly off (entities/sirens.ts; render/sirens.ts: long tail feathers down the rock, talons gripping it, wings folded close and lifting wide while one sings, a woman's face under long dark hair with a thin gold band at the brow, eyes that burn gold at night, the hair streaming back in flight). Their song is four notes in a minor key, D4, F4, A4 and C sharp 5 (audio/ambience.ts SIREN_NOTES, through the mermaids' voice with a slower, wider vibrato and a sag at each note's end), each drifting off toward the boat in dark violet, and near them their chord, D3 F3 A3 C sharp 4, takes the pad. Within 600 of the rocks (SIREN_REACH; full strength within 200) the song has the wheel: the bow is turned toward the nearest of their rocks at up to 1.6 radians a second and the boat kept moving at up to 90 (PULL_TURN, PULL_SPEED), and they sing one note after another while they have it. A boat going at least four fifths of its top speed with its bow away is half held (BREAK_FREE): steered hard away at full speed it breaks free, and the boat's own turning beats the pull. On their rocks while they hold them, the hull takes a knock of 10 every 1.5 s (main.js ROCK_KNOCK, KNOCK_EVERY), and a broken hull washes the boat up at the nearest hut (or by home) without its catch, as the other sinkings do, the message saying the sirens sang it onto the rocks and Washed up. Their arrival with the boat in earshot is a warning (Sail away, fast!), and their pull the first time in a while another. The mermaids' charm (part three): their song cannot pull a boat that wears it, and when it comes within 320 (CHARM_NEAR) they shriek and fly off for the rest of the night. A Sirens heard chip in the log and a page in the guide; on the radar they are threats. `mer.sirens` in the save.
- The Endless Isle, part one: the place (2026-10-23), island 13, the kid's (he called it Doom Island; the owner renamed it the Endless Isle the same day, after the endless battles between the pirates and the ninjas, and the name Doom Island goes to a separate island he will design next, so the code uses the new name throughout; through the owner, 2026-10-22: past the white buoys, two islands that mirror each other perfectly, pirates on one and ninjas on the other; why they fight, his answer: "to stimulate the economy"). The owner took the whole plan as it stood (2026-10-23; the plan is under the Endless Isle in the roadmap), and this is its first piece. `world/endless.ts` lays it out in its own frame, as Skull Island's is (u across the screen toward home, v down it), in the far far sea's left corner, the one corner still empty, a little in from its middle toward home: 357 of open water between it and the sea's far end on either side, its piers facing home. The channel runs along v = 0, the ninjas' half up the screen and the pirates' down it, so a fight will run up and down a phone's screen. Everything on a half is laid out once, in a half's own frame (u, w, w how far from the channel's middle), and the other half is its reflection, so the mirror is perfect by construction; a test walks a grid over both and finds the same land, heights and shallows on each. A half is a broad body along the channel, 1,280 across, its corners rounded, and a rounded end 1,040 out where its fort stands: a ring of wall 95 across with its gate toward the channel and the side's flag in the middle (the pirates' a log stockade with pointed tops and the Jolly Roger; the ninjas' white walls with dark tiled tops, red gateposts, and their banner, a throwing star on an indigo field; both flags the kid's: "Pirate flag - Jolly Roger ninja flag - throwing star on a field"). By each fort, its graveyard for the battles to come: the pirates' ship beached and heeled over, its mast broken, a scrap of sail on it; the ninjas' red shrine gate with a gong. Halfway down each half, its base for Hold the Forts in a ring: the pirates' Cannon Battery (a cannon on its carriage pointing at the channel, cannonballs stacked by it, on a ring of planks) and the ninjas' Bell Tower (four posts under a tiled roof, a bronze bell swinging, on flagstones). Off to the side of each far end, the same ground on each: the ninjas' snow peak (grey rock 330 high, snow down its top third in tongues, a snowfield round its foot) and the pirates' great tree (a huge trunk on spreading roots, a crown of green, four treehouses at three heights with a rope bridge between two, a rope ladder up the trunk, a crow's nest at the top flying the Jolly Roger). Both are drawn faded to a ghost while the figure, or the boat, is behind one and under it on the screen (render/endless.ts landmarkFade), so neither hides what is behind. Fourteen trees on each half in the same places, palms on the pirates', pines and blossom on the ninjas'. Trails join the pier, the fort, the base, the bridges and the shallows: sandy on the pirates', stepping stones on the ninjas'. The halves' ground is gold sand to jungle green on the pirates', pale stone to moss on the ninjas'. Across the channel (260 wide, deeper turquoise) stand two rope bridges, 330 either side of the middle, their planks ramped up to 6 at the middle and rope rails on posts; in the middle the sandbar with a wreck on it, its ribs showing, the Shipwreck base round it; round the sandbar the shallows, pale enough to see the bottom, running up under the beach on both sides: the figure wades them at half pace, in to the knees, rings round it (walker.ts WADE_PACE, WADE_SINK). The rest of the channel is deep, and hulls are kept out of it, off both halves and off the piers. A pier runs off each half toward home, with crates at its root where the hold is sold and a dock past the end (walker.ts ENDLESS_P_DOCK and ENDLESS_N_DOCK); landing on either, the figure can walk the whole island, both halves, over the bridges or through the shallows. Sighted from 900 off its edge, it says so (Land ho: the Endless Isle!, the mirror, the peak and the tree, battles that never end, which is how it got its name, and why they fight: to stimulate the economy), and the first time the camera pulls back for 6 s until both halves are on the screen. Landing, the first time in a while: whose half, and that nobody is about yet, both sides off getting ready for war. On the sea map, both halves in their own shapes, the ninjas' peak a white pit and the pirates' tree a green one; its arrow (a cutlass and a katana crossed, pulsing until it is found) points at the nearer dock, in the far far sea once island 11 is found; the log gets "Endless Isle found"; the save gets `endless.seen`. It is peaceful for now: the leaders, the minions and the battles come next, in parts.
- The Endless Isle, part two: the leaders and the minions (2026-10-23), the second piece of the owner's plan. At the foot of each pier, ashore on its channel side so the figure walks past him stepping off (`world/endless.ts` LEADER13, mirrored like the rest, 8 wide and in the way), stands his side's leader, the kid's names for them (his answer in his tab, 2026-10-23): Captain Cappy (big, a red coat, a black tricorn with a white feather, a cutlass) and Master Sensei (small and old, grey robes, a hood, a long white beard, a staff), drawn in `render/minions.ts`. Within 46 of one, the button says "Talk to Captain Cappy" or "Bow to Master Sensei", and his card opens (`#endlessCard`, parchment and red for the pirates, pale and indigo for the ninjas): what he says (`data/endlessfolk.ts` LEADER_SAYS: a hello, a different hello if you have joined the other side already, since you can fight for both, and his words once you are his), "Why are you fighting?" (to stimulate the economy: somebody has to buy all those cannonballs; throwing stars do not buy themselves), and the side to join: "Sign the articles: join the pirates" or "Bow: join the ninjas". Joined, the card says the minion is yours, and when it closes he comes. Jacks Parrow, the pirates' parrot (red and green, blue in his wings, an eyepatch), flies over from Captain Cappy to the figure's shoulder and squawks "EXPLETIVE DELETED!". He rides there (`entities/pals.ts` kind 'parrot', 21 up, 3.6 to the side), flies off to peck whatever the figure fights within reach, for 1 every 2 s, and back; nothing can hurt him or knock him out, he is up out of the way, and he is not in the way of what would hit the figure either. At sea he perches up the mast. Every 70 to 140 s ashore, soon after a landing, sometimes when the figure is hurt, and whenever he is tapped, he says one of his seven lines (the owner passed them with the plan; new ones go to her first), never the same twice running, in a bubble over him: each bleeped word a black bar with BLEEP on it and the censor's tone, each sworn one a red bar of symbols (@#$%!), with no real word under either. Bun-San, the ninjas' ninja-bun (a little blue bunny in a black ninja suit, a red headband, a tiny katana), comes out of a puff of smoke at the figure's heel and bows; he hops at its heel and spins in with his sword, for 1 every 1.5 s. Hit, he vanishes in a puff of smoke and pops out behind the figure, the way it came, half a second later, so he is never worn out; tap him and he flourishes his sword. At sea he sits on the bow. Both are friends like the rest: on the roll-call board at the castle (they wait in the courtyard, the parrot on the flagstones), in the army ashore (the bun, the parrot being on the shoulder), in towers and on the tar island. The landing message now points you at the leader. The save keeps the sides joined (`endless.pirate`, `endless.ninja`); the tester can join either. The minions do nothing more for now: in the battles to come, only your side's fights.
- The Endless Isle, part three: Capture the Flag (2026-10-23), the third piece of the owner's plan. Joined to a side, its leader's card has "Fight: Capture the Flag" (`#endlessFight`), and you fight for that side, five a side: you and four bots against five bots (`entities/ctf.ts`, its numbers in `data/battle.ts`, the table under Tuning tables). Each team is a tank, a healer and three damage, you in a damage place, its names the side's (the pirates' Quartermaster, Ship's Cook and Swashbucklers, with names like Barnacle Bob and Mad Molly; the ninjas' Stone Guard, Blossom Monk and Shadows, Whisper, Pebble and the rest). The two sides have the same numbers and only look different (`render/crew.ts`: the pirates red, the ninjas blue, a ring at every fighter's feet in its side's colour; the tank bigger with a shield, the healer throwing a fish or a blossom, the damage with a cutlass or a katana). Your armour and spear stay outside: you have your role's eight hearts and its sword on the blade button (2 a hit, from 40, every 0.7 s), and your companions sit it out, all but your side's minion, who fights the other side's fighters. Everyone starts in their own fort behind its gate, shut with bars for 10 s (you are kept inside it); then the gates drop and the clock runs from 7:00. Touch their flag, in the middle of their fort, and it is on your back, and you walk at 85%; bring it to your own flag and it scores, but only while yours is at home. A carrier beaten drops it where they fell; a flag left alone goes home after 10 s, and at once if its own side touches it. Beaten, a fighter gets up at its side's graveyard 6 s later, whole (you wait there flickering, still). Both flags out a minute, and each carrier takes a heart more from every hit, so nobody hides with one forever. First to three wins; on the clock the most wins, the same a draw. The bots find their way on the island's own grids (`world/endlessnav.ts`: where a fighter stands or wades, worked out from the island's frame so it mirrors exactly, and the ways across it, a coarse field of how far every cell is from a place): the tank defends, the healer escorts a carrier or the team, the damage attack or hold the middle; only the defender chases a thief, never more than two of them go for you unless you have their flag, and the side you fight against strikes a little slower (1.12), so a kid wins a bit more than half the time. Bots alone, 40 battles: each side wins about as often, about 2.3 flags scored a battle, about 40% drawn (a test keeps the sides even, most battles decided and flags being scored; a respawn of 10 s rather than the plan's 6 brings the draws down to about a quarter, if battles with the kid draw too often). A score bar along the top, where the order card is (`#ctfBar`: the score in each side's colour, the clock, and what is happening: the gates, back up in so many, you have their flag, they have yours); arrows at the screen's edge to their flag, home with it, and to yours when it is out; your hearts show, the boat's hull and the snacks hide (no eating in a battle). Over, `#ctfCard`: who won, the score, the leader's word (`data/endlessfolk.ts` LEADER_AFTER), what you did (flags scored, carried and sent home, foes beaten, battles won as your role), and the pay, the plan's 500 for a win and 200 for a loss (a draw pays and marks as a loss; the plan has none), then Fight again or Done (back by the leader). Leaving early pays nothing: "Leave the battle" sits in the score bar, out of the way of the thumb that walks, and asks "Leave? Tap again" first (the pill at the bottom left, where the owner's thumb went to walk, left a battle at a touch; fixed 2026-10-23, and at her word that pill moved to the top left everywhere, under the coins, the order card and the charter making room while it shows); going aboard leaves too. The save keeps the battles fought and won and the marks for each side and role (`endless.battles`), 2 for a win and 1 otherwise, for the badges that come next; the tester can win the battle you are in. The leaders' "joined" words now ask you to battle. Honest note: the balance is the bots' against each other; how it feels with the kid on one side is untested until he plays, and every number is in `data/battle.ts`.
- The Endless Isle, part four: roles and Badge I (2026-10-23), the plan's fourth piece. "Fight: Capture the Flag" now opens the badge card in the leader's card (`#endlessRoles`): the leader's word on picking (`data/endlessfolk.ts` LEADER_PICK), and the three places in his crew, each with its hearts and its Badge I ability, by the side's names (the pirates' Quartermaster, Swashbuckler and Ship's Cook; the ninjas' Stone Guard, Shadow and Blossom Monk). You fight in the place you pick, a bot in each of the others (`entities/ctf.ts`, its new `yourRole`; you the tank, one of your side's damage bots guards the flag). Badge I is yours from your first battle in that place, so every battle has its ability; Badges II and III come with marks in a later piece. The abilities (`data/battle.ts` ABILITY, the plan's numbers, the same on both sides, under Tuning tables): the tank's shout ("Oi! Over here!", the Mountain Shout) makes every enemy within 140 come at you for 4 s, a carrier too, and a ring spreads round you; the damage's shot (the Flintlock's bang, the Shuriken's two stars) aims at the nearest enemy within 300, a carrier first, a red dashed line for 0.4 s while it aims, then hits for 4; the healer's heal (a Sea Biscuit, a cup of Green Tea, flying over) gives 4 hearts to the most hurt teammate within 220, or you. Each aims itself; on the round button above the blade (`#ability`, the side's colour, the time still to wait swept dark over it) or E; nobody to use it on, it says so. The bots use theirs: a healer once a teammate near is three hearts down, a shot only at the foe a bot is already after and not yet in its sword's reach (shooting every carrier in sight stalled the battles: 0.7 flags scored a battle and two thirds drawn), a shout once two foes are close or one is after a teammate or carries our flag. And late on (2:30 left), a side not ahead sends its middle in after the flag too. Bots alone over 60 battles: even sides, 1.85 flags a battle, a little under 40% drawn. The healer's basic attack is thrown, from 160; the tank's 14 hearts show as a bar in a battle, as the plan says, since fourteen hearts do not fit on a phone. The results card counts the hearts a healer healed. Fight again keeps your place.
- The Endless Isle, part five: the Ninja Slalom, one run (2026-10-23), the plan's fifth piece, a break from fighting. A chairlift stands at the foot of the ninjas' peak on its channel side (`world/endless.ts` LIFT13, drawn in `render/endless.ts`: a dark wood station under a red roof, its cable up the face to a hut on the top, chairs going up one side and down the other; the pirates' great tree has its rope ladder on its front instead, so it is not mirrored, and nothing in the mirror test changes). Anyone who has landed can ride it ("Ride the chairlift"), the plan's call. The run is seen from just behind the skier as it streams toward you, the drone's into-the-screen view on snow (`entities/slalom.ts`, drawn by `render/slalom.ts`): a piste between banks of pines, far white peaks, and the skier from behind in your jacket's colour (the boat's paint, as the figure's shirt is), a red headband's tails flying and a sword on the back. The thumb carves; pushed up it tucks for speed, pulled back it slows. Gates are pairs of red or blue panels with a line of dye across the snow between them; one gone round adds 2 s. Pines, rocks and snowmen are clear of the line through the gates (a test checks); hit one and the skier rolls up in a ball of snow for 1.5 s and sets off again slowly, about 2 s lost, never out. Ice slides you on sideways (the skis answer a fifth as fast), moguls slow you to three quarters. Three ramps throw you about a second into the air: Trick! (`#skiTrick`, bottom right, pulsing gold while you are up, or Space) turns a flip, or a spin if you are steering as you tap; landed it is a second off, still turning on landing it is a tumble. Paper lanterns float over the run a nudge off the line; pass within 1.5 m and the sword slices it, 10 coins each (as the drone's rings pay; my pick, hers to change). The clock, the run's name and how far down are along the top, under Stop the run (the way out, top left, as everywhere now); the lanterns sliced at the right. A first run in a session opens on how it goes, four lines, and Trick! skips it; then 3, 2, 1, Go. Over the finish banner the skier stops in a spray and a card gives the time, against your best, with the gates, lanterns and tricks; the time is kept (`data/slalom.ts` skied, saved as `endless.slalom`), the lanterns paid. Stopped partway, the lanterns are paid and the time is not kept. A bot down the line makes all 16 gates in about 56 s, 42 s tucked; straight down misses 15 and comes in 30 s slower. The Fox Run, the Doom Drop, the medals and the snow monster are part ten.
- The Endless Isle, part six: Badges II and III (2026-10-24), the plan's sixth piece. The marks part four began counting (2 a win, 1 a draw or a loss, for the side and the place you fought in) now earn badges: Badge II at 3 marks, Badge III at 8 (`data/battle.ts` BADGE_MARKS, badgeOf), each with one more ability (ABILITIES, three a place, the same numbers on both sides, under Tuning tables). The tank's guard (Batten the Hatches, Iron Skin) halves the hits on it for 6 s: a ship's wheel held up as a shield, or skin gone grey as stone. Its stomp (Drop Anchor, Earthquake Stomp) holds it still in a red ring for 0.6 s, then every foe within 90 takes 2 and is stunned for 1.5 s (stars round their heads): the anchor slammed down, or the ground cracking. The damage's leap (Swing In, Shadow Step) takes it to a foe within 220, down just past it, and strikes for 3: on a rope, or out of smoke. Its bomb (Powder Keg, Firework Bomb) is thrown at a foe within 250, the one where it will catch the most (a carrier first), lands in 0.35 s in a red ring 45 across, and bursts 1.2 s later for 5 on every foe still in it: the ring is the warning, and stepping out of it the counter. The healer's heal over time (Lime Ration, Cherry Blossom) gives the most hurt teammate within 220, or you, a heart a second for 6 s, lime slices or cherry petals round it. Its group heal is the one ability each side does its own way, the owner's plan's example: the pirates' Sea Shanty is sung, a heart a second for 6 s to every teammate within 180 of the singer, notes rising and the tune played on the mermaids' four notes, the singer at half pace; the ninjas' Lotus Circle is a glowing ring left on the ground, a heart a second for 6 s to every teammate in it. Stunned or winding up, a fighter cannot strike or use an ability; a guarded hit can be half a heart, so hearts now show halves (½). Each badge's ability has its own round button in an arc round the blade (`#ability` above it, `#ability2` and `#ability3` round to its left, each with its icon and its wait swept dark) and its own key (E, R, F); in a battle only your badges' buttons show. Everyone in a battle has as many badges as you have in your place (`entities/ctf.ts` badge), my pick: a Badge III battle is Badge III on both sides, so a new badge brings new things to dodge as well as to use. The bots use their newest first, by rules of their own (botWants: a guard once a foe is on it and it is three down, a stomp once two foes are round it or one carries our flag, a leap at its foe out of sword's reach, a bomb that will catch two or a carrier, a heal over time once a teammate is two down with none on it, a group heal once two near are two down). Bots alone, 100 battles at each badge: Badge I 28 pirate wins, 38 ninja, 34 drawn, 2.01 flags a battle; Badge II 27, 35, 38, 2.14; Badge III 37, 29, 34, 2.25. A new badge stops the end of the battle for its ceremony, the plan's (`#ctfBadge`, before `#ctfResults`): the medal spins in with the badge's numeral, ringed in the side's colour, your place named, the leader's proud line (`data/endlessfolk.ts` LEADER_BADGE), on the pirates' side Jacks Parrow's squawk too, one of the approved seven, bleeped as he says it; then the new ability played once on a little canvas (`render/battlefx.ts` drawBadgeShow, you and whoever it is for, 2.8 s; tap it to see it again), and what it does, on which button and key. "Brilliant! On to the results" opens the results, which now say your marks in that place and what the next badge takes. The badge card in the leader's card shows each place's badge, its abilities so far and what the newest does, and how many marks the next badge takes. Gold badges at 20 marks, the plan's last rung, come with the side rewards (piece eleven). No change to the save: the marks were saved from part four.
- Capture the Flag, kinder, and a tank worth the name (2026-10-24), the owner's call after playing the healer: "I only do 1pt of damage but they do 2-3pts of damage and I die super fast". She was right on both counts. The tank and the healer hit for 1 while three of the five on the other side hit for 2, and the tank, the plan's 1-a-hit, died more than anyone in a battle (9.4 times in 60 bot battles, the damage 6.6): its shout pulled foes onto it and it had nothing to take them with, and a damage beat it one on one. Her pick of the two fixes offered, both: the tank hits for 2, swinging every 1.2 s (every 1.0 s left the bots' battles short of flags and lopsided over the tests' 40), and has armour (`data/battle.ts` ARMOUR): every hit on a tank is a heart less, never under half, after a guard has halved it. And you have the same armour in any place, once even as the tank (twice would make a fourteen-heart tank all but unbeatable): the other side's 2s are 1s on you, their healers' throws half a heart, a keg 4. The bots fight each other at full strength. Bots alone after it, 100 battles at each badge: the tank is beaten 6.3 times a battle at Badge I, 7.9 at II, 6.8 at III, the fewest at I and III; the damage 11.5, 8.5 and 10.5. The heart row draws half a heart now, as armour leaves them.
- The healer's throw, seen and reaching (2026-10-24), the owner, playing the healer again: "My throw has like zero range". It reached 160, four swords, but did not look it: the button showed the blade, the fish was a pale speck, and 160 is under half the screen, so foes in plain sight were out of reach and the button simply did not come. Now the button shows a throw (a fish on a dashed arc) for a healer; the cook's fish is a blue-grey fish with a tail and an eye, the monk's a pink mochi on its leaf, each with its shadow under it and, the owner's ask ("And sparkles. Bc magic fish"), a trail of twinkling stars behind it, gold and white for the fish, pink and white for the mochi, and a little burst of them where it lands; and the reach is 220, the heals' own, her pick of three (keeping 160 or going to the shot's 300 the others). The bots' healers throw from as far. Bots alone after it, 100 battles at each badge: 26/37/37, 32/35/33, 35/37/28 (pirate wins, ninja, drawn), 1.6 to 2.4 flags a battle; healers beaten 8.2, 5.5 and 8.4 times a battle. The red laser (the shot's aim line) she found deadly; offered a longer, dodgeable aim, a weaker or a rarer shot, she kept it as it is, with your armour taking it to 3.
- The Endless Isle, part seven: Hold the Forts (2026-10-24), the plan's seventh piece, its second battle. The leader's card now offers both, "Fight: Capture the Flag" and "Fight: Hold the Forts" (`#endlessForts`), each opening the badge card, the leader's word on picking its own for this one (`data/endlessfolk.ts` LEADER_PICK_FORTS). It is the same battle on the same island, the same five a side, roles, badges, abilities, armour and gates (`entities/ctf.ts`, its `mode`), with three bases instead of the flags, which stay in their forts (`world/endless.ts` HOLDS13): the pirates' Cannon Battery and the ninjas' Bell Tower, each on its own half and the mirror of the other, and the Shipwreck on the sandbar between, reached by wading, its ring wider (66, WRECK_RING) for the wreck lying in it. Nobody holds any at the start. A side alone in a ring takes it in 5 s; both sides in it, nothing moves; a side taking another's first undoes what the other had done of it; a taking left alone fades at half pace, at once with its own side back in it. Every second a side scores 1, 3 or 10 for holding one, two or three, and the first to 400 wins, or the most when the 6-minute clock runs out (`data/battle.ts` FORTS, the plan's numbers, the clock mine). The bots: the tank holds the Shipwreck, one damage its own base, one the Shipwreck, one raids theirs (then the Shipwreck, then home, whichever is not yet held), and the healer goes with the nearest teammate; near its base a bot fights whoever is in or by the ring, and on the way only what comes at it (the defender not at all away from home). On the ground each ring is tinted its holder's colour, a band round it filling with the taker's colour as it is taken, white dashes while both sides are in it, the holder's flag by it (`render/battlefx.ts` drawHoldRing). Under the score, three chips (Cannon, Wreck, Bell) in their holders' colours, pulsing while one is being taken, and a line on what you are doing ("Taking it: 3", "Holding it", "Clear them out of it!"); arrows at the screen's edge to the bases not yours; a message when one is taken, either way, and when they start taking one of yours. The results count the bases you took. The same pay and marks as Capture the Flag. Bots alone, 60 battles at each badge: 31/29, 33/27 and 28/32 pirate and ninja wins, none drawn; about 3 1/3 minutes a battle (the plan's 3 to 5); bases taken 6.5, 9.7 and 11.4 times a battle. And the message now goes below the score bar in a battle, which had grown taller than the place it went.
- The Endless Isle, part eight: the Treehouse Climb, its first stage (2026-10-24), the plan's eighth piece, the kid's Donkey Kong ("gorillas roll the barrels down the treehouses"). At the foot of the pirates' great tree, on its front where its rope ladder hangs (`world/endless.ts` TREE13, the pirate half only, as the chairlift is the ninjas' only), anyone who has landed gets "Climb the great tree", and the view turns side on, a stage a screen (`entities/treehouse.ts`, the stage in `data/treehouse.ts`, drawn by `render/treehouse.ts`): inside the tree, its trunk up the middle, leaves round the edges and sky between, plank beams lashed across it and rope ladders between them, the gorilla at the top beside its stack of barrels, beating its chest and reaching for the next one, and the hut on the ledge at the very top flying the Jolly Roger. The climber is you, side on, in your hair, skin and the boat's paint for a shirt, as the skier is, seen from behind on a ladder. The arrows (`#thPad`, bottom left) are one pad a thumb slides round, eight ways, so up and to the right walks you on and up a ladder as you reach it; Jump (`#thJump`, bottom right) or Space jumps; the keys walk and climb too, and so does the floating stick if a thumb lands on the stage instead. The stage is scaled to the width, or to show at least two thirds of it, and rides up with the climber, eased so it does not bob with each jump, kept above the arrows however tall the phone's bottom edge is. Barrels zigzag down the six planks, each sloping the other way to the one below, dropping off each one's low end, now and then down a ladder (more often when you are somewhere below it, but seldom down the one you are on, where there is no getting out of the way), and out of the tree at the bottom. Jumped, a barrel is 5 coins; one that reaches you costs a heart of three, a second dazed, then a while blinking when barrels go through you; with all three gone the stage starts again from the bottom, the coins kept: there is no game over, the plan's. Two belaying pins float a jump up (walking under one leaves it, as an 8 s pin you could not climb with would otherwise trap you), and with one in hand for 8 s every barrel within reach is smashed, 10 coins each, but you cannot climb or jump, as with Donkey Kong's hammer. On the ledge at the top the clock stops, the climber walks in at the door, a card gives the time against your best, with the barrels jumped and smashed and the coins, and 100 coins more are paid on the way down; the climb and its time are kept (`data/treehouse.ts` climbed, saved as `endless.climbs`). Climbing down early pays what the barrels made, and is not kept unless it was more than a few seconds in. A first climb in a session opens on how it goes, four lines (Jump skips it), then Ready, then Climb! The first layout had its ladders close together and a bot was at the top in 14 s having met three barrels, so the ladders now go up at alternate ends, as Donkey Kong's do, and the way up walks the length of each plank into the barrels coming at you, with a short cut halfway along two planks. A bot that jumps the barrels rolling at it tops the stage in all of 60 tries, in about 28 s, losing about one heart and jumping four or five barrels a climb; one that never jumps loses all three hearts about seven times before it gets there. My picks, hers to change: 5, 10 and 100 coins; a hit costs a heart, not a fall back down; the pin's 8 s. The Rope Swings, the Bucket Lifts and the Crow's Nest are part nine.
- The Endless Isle, part nine, its first stage: the Rope Swings (2026-10-24), the treehouse's second stage, as the plan has it: "swing over gaps, cross sagging rope bridges, and dodge falling coconuts" (`data/treehouse.ts` ROPE_SWINGS; the engine, `entities/treehouse.ts`, grew ropes, rope bridges and coconuts, the Barrel Planks unchanged, its tests untouched). In at the Barrel Planks' door at the top, the climb goes on up into the Rope Swings, its own how it goes first; at the top of the last stage built, down you come. Two platforms each side of a gap too wide for a running jump (a test checks), a rope swinging over each gap from a stub of branch: jump at one and your hands catch it (5 coins, once a climb); up and down slide you along it, no higher than four tenths of the way up; Jump lets go, flying on the way it is swinging, with a little hop. Let go over the gap and you drop to the level below, unhurt; the rope you let go of cannot be caught again till you land (a bot caught it again mid-hop and swung forever). Two rope bridges across the tree: slats on ropes with a hand rope on posts, sagging at rest, and dipping where you stand, deepest mid-span, the dip going with you as you walk, easing in as you land. Under the planks above them hang coconut bunches: every few seconds one wobbles for 0.9 s, then drops and splats on the first plank it meets; on your head it costs a heart, as a barrel does. The climber hangs from its hands along the rope as it swings. Each stage's time is kept as you get to its top (`timed`, the best and the highest stage; climbing down counts one climb), and once you have climbed one, "Climb the great tree" opens a card (`#treeCard`) to start at any stage reached, the stages above shut, still to reach or still being built. A bot that catches each rope at the near end of its swing, lets go at the far end, and waits for a wobbling coconut ahead tops the stage in all of 40 tries, in about 64 s, a coconut on the head every other climb. The Bucket Lifts and the Crow's Nest come next.
- The Endless Isle, part nine, its second stage: the Bucket Lifts (2026-10-24), the treehouse's third stage, as the plan has it: "pulley lifts going up and down, and levers that lower bridges (the puzzle part)" (`data/treehouse.ts` BUCKET_LIFTS; the engine grew lifts, levers and drawbridges, the first two stages unchanged, their tests untouched). In at the Rope Swings' door, on up into it. Two lifts, a rope of wooden buckets going round a wheel at each end, up the left side and down the right: stand where a bucket comes up out of the ground and it scoops you up, or step off a ledge's end into one passing below; you can walk about in it, walk off its edge onto a ledge, and if you stay in to the end of its run it tips you out as it goes over the wheel. Three ledges, the first and the top one each with a drawbridge in the middle, pulled up on a chain, no floor till it is all the way down (walk off its hinge and you drop). Walk into a lever (10 coins) and its bridge comes down over 1.2 s. The way up is the puzzle: up the left lift to the first ledge and its lever, over its bridge and up a ladder to the second ledge's lever, which lets down the top ledge's bridge; then down the right lift and up the left one again, all the way, to the top ledge, and over to the door. Without the second lever the top bridge's gap is too wide to jump (a test checks). Coconuts drop here too, three bunches, a little slower. Out of tries, the levers go back up with everything else. The stage card's middle line counts the levers pulled. A bot that knows the way and waits for a wobbling coconut just ahead tops the stage in all of 30 tries, in about 47 s, a coconut on the head about one climb in eight. My picks, hers to change: 10 coins a lever; the levers stay pulled through a coconut on the head, but not when you run out of tries. The Crow's Nest comes next.
- The Endless Isle, part nine, its third stage: the Crow's Nest (2026-10-24), the treehouse's fourth and last, as the plan has it: "knock out the eight pegs, the barrel-roller tumbles into the sea with a splash, and the pirates' treasure chest is yours" (`data/treehouse.ts` CROWS_NEST; the engine grew pegs, holes and monkeys, the first three stages unchanged and their tests untouched, but for a test reading the door, which the last stage has none of, and a belaying pin still in hand put down at the top of any stage). Donkey Kong's rivets. In at the Bucket Lifts' door, out at the very top of the tree, in the open over the crown, the sea far below to the horizon with the ninjas' isle and its snow peak out on it: four decks round the trunk, which thins to a mast, narrower going up, over a full-width one on the crown's leaves, ten rope ladders between them, and on top of the mast the crow's nest, a round tub with the gorilla in it, the pirates' chest beside it and the Jolly Roger over it, out of reach. Each deck is held by two pegs near its ends, glowing a little: walk over one (or jump low over it) and it pops out, 10 coins, leaving a hole 12 wide, which is not under you as you knock it out but is on the way back, so it is jumped or dropped through to the deck below, unhurt. The bar counts the pegs still in. The gorilla tosses monkeys down onto the top deck, two about at first and a third after 30 s, the threat in place of Donkey Kong's firefoxes: each knuckle-walks its deck at about half your speed, turns at an end or a hole, now and then toward you, and takes a ladder it comes to, up or down, about a third of the time. Walked into, a monkey costs a heart; jumped, 5 coins; come down on its head and you bounce off it and it runs the other way (a test of a too-early standing jump landed on one and lost a heart, which is no way to treat a forgiving jump); a belaying pin (two, as on the Barrel Planks) bonks one off the tree, 10 coins. With every peg out the clock stops and 100 coins are paid for the top; the crow's nest shakes, tips over (the view rides up to it a moment), the gorilla tumbles out and down past everything, SPLASH into the sea, and the chest drops to land by you and opens, heaped with gold: 1,000 coins the first time (no new save field: the first time is told by the highest stage climbed), 250 every time after; the card says so, and the walk down says the treasure was among the coins. Out of tries, the pegs are back in and the monkeys gone. A bot that goes for the nearest peg, up and down the nearest ladder (waiting while a monkey is on it), jumping holes and monkeys coming at it, tops the stage in all of 40 tries, in about 30 s, losing under a heart a climb; one that never jumps a monkey does too, losing about one and a half, three times in 40 out of tries and starting over. My picks, hers to change: monkeys for the hazard (the plan named none) and the bounce off their heads; 10 coins a peg; the chest's 1,000 and 250, which pays well the first time and stays below fishing after. With it the Treehouse Climb is whole, part nine done.
- The Endless Isle, part ten, its first half: the Fox Run and the medals (2026-10-24), the plan's tenth piece, "Ninja Slalom, all three runs. Medals, and a snow monster" (`data/slalom.ts` RUNS.fox, RUN_ORDER, medalOf; the engine takes each run's steepness and its mix of what is in the gaps, the Bunny Slope laid out exactly as before, its tests untouched). Each run now has a gold, a silver and a bronze time: the clock's bar shows all three under it, each dimming once the clock is past it, and over the finish the card says which medal the time won, or how far off bronze it was; the walk down says so too, for a medal better than that run's best had. Ski the Bunny Slope to the end and the Fox Run opens (the walk down says so): a blue run, 880 m to the Bunny Slope's 620, its piste 14 m wide to 16, its gates 4.2 m to 5, swinging further side to side and closer together, four jumps, more ice, and 15% steeper, quicker every way, down it and across it as you carve; in the late afternoon, its sky gold and its haze peach, the snow fading back to white near you. Once the Bunny Slope has been skied, "Ride the chairlift" opens a card at the top (`#skiCard`): each run with its piste marker (a green circle, a blue square, a black diamond on a white sign), its best time and medal, a run shut until the one before it has been skied to the end, and the Doom Drop still being groomed. The medal times come from runs down the racing line: tucked all the way is gold, half tucked silver, cruising bronze, braking nothing, on both runs (a test checks each); tricks take a second each off, so a tucked run with a tumble can still make gold with a trick or two. My picks, hers to change: the times (the Bunny Slope 44, 50 and 62 s, the Fox Run 54, 62 and 76), the Fox Run opening after one run down the Bunny Slope, and the white snow-ninja outfit for gold on all three runs left for the side rewards, piece eleven. The Doom Drop and its yeti come next.
- The Endless Isle, part ten, its second half: the Doom Drop and the yeti (2026-10-24), the rest of the plan's tenth piece, and the kid's answer, "a yeti chases you down the black run" (`data/slalom.ts` RUNS.doom; `entities/slalom.ts` chase). Ski the Fox Run to the end and the Doom Drop opens (the walk down says so), the black run: 1,000 m, its piste 13 m wide, its gates 4 m and 25 to 31 m apart, five jumps, more in the gaps, and a quarter steeper than the Bunny Slope; at dusk, its sky slate blue and its haze grey. A yeti comes down after you. It starts 14 m behind and roars at the go (the Reaper's roar, and "Go! The yeti is after you!"), sets off 1.2 s later, and comes at a little under the run's cruise when it is close and a little over when it is far back, so a clean run pulls away from it but it is never far, and every tumble or slow patch lets it gain. It growls as it comes within 12 m. Catching you on the snow (within 1.2 m) it swipes you into a tumble, the usual 2 s or so lost ("The yeti got you!"), and stops 2.2 s to roar, by which time you are up and away; catching you already tumbling, or in the air, it only roars. It is never drawn on the run itself: its face rises from the bottom of the screen from 26 m back, the nearer the higher, white fur edged blue so it shows on the snow, its paws reaching up either side once it is close, roaring with its fangs bare when it has just set off or swiped; and a chip under the lanterns says how far back it is, red under 15 m. The walk down says whether you outran it, or how many times it caught you. A bot down the line makes all 33 gates and is never caught, in 76 s, or 61 s tucked (tucked, the tightest pair or two go by too quick to make); the yeti comes within about 6 m as you get going, then falls back, 23 m behind at the end cruising, 148 m tucked; braking all the way, it catches you again and again (tests check each). The words over the run now start just under the bars, on every run, so they no longer cross the clock's bar or the yeti's chip. My picks, hers to change: the medal times (62, 70 and 86 s), the yeti's speeds, and a swipe costing a tumble and no more. All three runs are built; the white snow-ninja outfit for gold on all three is piece eleven's, the side rewards. No change to the save: each run's best time was already kept under its key.
- The Garden Isle, part one: the place (2026-10-24), island 14, the owner's ("a garden isle. We want to replicate this game", the Roblox game Grow a Garden, its loop rebuilt with our own crops, weather and coins; the plan, with her calls, is under the Garden Isle in the roadmap), and the first of its eleven pieces. `world/garden.ts` lays it out in its own frame like Skull Island's (u across the screen toward home, v down it): in the far deep's left corner (-x, +y), the last empty corner of the far deep, straight across the map from island 10, 6,300 from home along the line to the Endless Isle and 420 up the screen off it, so the run out to the battles passes its dock; round, 340 across give or take a gentle wander, clear of the far deep's marlin schools by more than their width (a test checks every school). It is drawn by `render/garden.ts`: shallows in rings, a ring of sand, lush grass with lighter patches, sandy paths from the pier's root to the plot and each stall. Its pier runs out 210 toward home, with crates beside its root where the hold sells and a dock ring past its end (`GARDEN_DOCK`); step ashore and the figure walks the island. By the pier is the plot, edged with low planks the figure steps over (`PLOT14`): 24 beds four across and six down (`BEDS`), the six nearest the pier dug, dark soil in furrows, the other 18 staked out with string for when the plot grows, butterflies over the dug ones. Round it stand the seed stall (a green-and-cream awning, seed packets along it), the sell stand (a blue one, empty baskets on the counter) and the gear shed (a red roof, a watering can by its door), all three shut with a cloth down or the door closed; a windmill turning at the back, eight fruit trees, apples and oranges, and a scarecrow over the staked-out corner. The stalls, the windmill and the trees are solid; the plot is not. Sighted from 800 off its shore the first time ("Land ho: the Garden Isle!"), it is found: a log chip, its shape on the sea map and the radar, and a little green sprout marker at the screen's edge once the far deep is open and the boat is past the buoys. Ashore, the plot and each stall say what they are once a landing; nothing grows yet. The save gets `garden: {seen}`, left out until it is sighted (`data/garden.ts`, where the beds, basket and stock will go).
- The Garden Isle, part two: first harvest (2026-10-24), the second piece of the owner's plan: Grow a Garden's loop works end to end. The seed stall opens ("Look at the seeds" at its front): a card like the old man's store with the three crops the stall always has, carrot, strawberry and blueberry, each with what it does, its tier and how many you hold, a seed a tap (99 of a kind at most; `data/garden.ts` CROPS, the table under Tuning tables). Ashore on the isle a seed tray sits above the action button: a chip for each seed held, the chosen one green, and the basket, so many of 50 (`ui/garden.ts`). Stand on one of the six dug beds and the action button reads it: "Plant a carrot" on an empty bed (the tray's seed), "Carrot: ripe in 50 s" while it grows, "Pick 3 strawberries" once ripe, and "No seeds: the seed stall has them" with none held; the staked-out beds do nothing yet. It grows in real time, closed or open (the owner's call): a bed keeps when it was planted, how many fruit are ripe on it and when the next one ripens (milliseconds, Date.now()'s), and works out what has ripened whenever it is looked at, so the game closed for a night has grown on by a night. A carrot is ripe after a minute and picked once, freeing its bed; a strawberry first fruits after two minutes and then one a minute, a blueberry after four and then one every two, and each holds four ripe and then waits, so a week away pays no more than a night and nothing withers. Picking a full plant starts its next fruit from then. A phone clock set back never makes a fruit wait longer than its first growing time. Ashore, a growing bed has a little pill over it with its time to go and a ripe one twinkles; the plants grow from a sprout to full size, the carrot's orange shoulder showing at the soil when it is ripe, the strawberries and blueberries going from pale to red or blue on the plant as each ripens (`render/garden.ts` gardenPlantSolids). The sell stand opens too: "Sell your basket for 15" sells the lot. The gear shed stays shut. Coins are the boat's coins; at these three crops the garden is a trickle beside the boat by design, and the rarer seeds of the next piece are where it starts to pay.
- The Garden Isle, part three: every seed and the dawn restock (2026-10-24), the plan's third piece. The stall now has twelve crops in six tiers, common to divine (`data/garden.ts` CROPS, the table under Tuning tables): carrot and strawberry, blueberry and sweet potato, tomato and sweetcorn, pineapple and papaya, coconut palm and dragon fruit, the lantern gourd and the kraken squash, the last two ours where Grow a Garden's top crops are its own. Its stock comes in at every dawn, as Grow a Garden's does every five minutes (a day here is five minutes of play): rolled the first time the stall is opened on a new day (`stockToday`, keyed to the game's day counter) and saved at once, so a reload cannot roll it again; each crop is in on its odds, the carrot, strawberry, blueberry and tomato always, as in theirs, the rest from one day in three down to one in 120, with so many seeds (8 to 12 carrots, a single seed of the top ones). The card shows every crop, its tier on a coloured chip, how many are left today, and greys out what is sold out or not in. Seeds are bought from the day's stock. The prices are the owner's "rival the boat" (2026-10-24), held by a test (`GUARD`): a full plot of 24 beds of the best crop that keeps fruiting, left overnight, pays 30,000 to 50,000 (the lantern gourd, 24 beds holding three each at 560: 40,320); tended by hand no crop pays more than 2,000 a minute, about the boat; every crop pays its seed back, once picked or in at most five dozen picks; and each tier is dearer than the one below, and from legendary up rarer. The throttle is the stall, as in theirs: a lantern gourd seed is in about one day in 80. Each crop is drawn growing, from a sprout to its full plant with its fruit on it: the sweet potato's vine and purple tuber, the tomato on its stake, sweetcorn's tall stalk and cobs, the pineapple in its spiky rosette, papaya and coconut palm as little trees, the dragon fruit's climbing cactus and pink fruit, the lantern gourd's curling vine, and the kraken squash swelling purple as its vines curl like tentacles. The tall ones keep to their beds. At night the open stalls are lit, each ripe bed has a small light so what to pick can be found in the dark, and a ripe lantern gourd glows wide and warm, like the lanternfish (`gardenLights`). The seed tray scrolls sideways when many kinds are held, the basket always first.
- The Garden Isle, part four: size and giants (2026-10-24), the plan's fourth piece. Every fruit, as it ripens, has a size of its own: between 0.8 and 1.2 of normal, and one in 200 a giant, three to four times normal (`data/garden.ts` SIZE, sizeOf). It is worked out from when its plant was planted and how many fruit the plant has ripened, so it is the same however often it is looked at, and a reload cannot roll it again; a plant keeps the sizes of its ripe fruit and its count (`w`, `born`). What a fruit sells for goes with the square of its size, as in Grow a Garden, never under 0.9 squared (`fruitValue`), and it weighs its crop's normal weight times its size (a carrot 0.2 kg, a kraken squash 9). Grow a Garden's sizes run 0.7 to 1.4; ours are narrower so that size adds about a tenth to what a plot earns and the owner's guardrails still hold, which their test now checks with size in (a full plot of lantern gourds overnight about 44,000). Ripe fruit are drawn at their size on the plant, a giant up to 2.2 times as big so it keeps to its bed; picking one says its weight ("Picked a carrot, 0.21 kg."), and picking a giant is a rare catch, with its own fanfare and toast ("A giant pineapple! 6.00 kg. It sells for 67,200."). No one fruit sells for more than 100,000 (`FRUIT_MAX`, twice the dearest seed): a giant kraken squash, 60,000 by up to 23 for its size, would otherwise pay more than a million. The basket keeps each fruit's size (`bag`), so the stand pays for each as it is; a basket saved before sizes comes in as normal-sized fruit.
- The Garden Isle, part five: Gold and Rainbow (2026-10-24), the plan's fifth piece, at Grow a Garden's own numbers (the owner's call): as each fruit ripens, one in 100 is Gold and sells for 20 times as much, and one in 1,000 is Rainbow and sells for 50 times; never both (`data/garden.ts` VARIANTS, variantOf). Like its size it is worked out from its plant and its count, so a reload cannot roll it again; the plant keeps it for each ripe fruit (`v`) and the basket for each fruit picked. It multiplies on top of size, and the most one fruit sells for is still 100,000. A Gold fruit is drawn gold and a Rainbow one runs through every colour, each with a sparkle over it (`render/garden.ts`); picking one is a rare catch, with its own fanfare and toast saying what it is and what it sells for ("A Rainbow tomato! 0.20 kg. It sells for 3,000."), the rarest of what was picked named first, Rainbow before Gold before a giant. Gold and Rainbow add about a quarter to what a plot earns, on top of the tenth that size adds, so to keep the owner's guardrail (a full plot overnight no more than 50,000) the lantern gourd sells for 500, not 560: a full plot of them overnight now comes to about 49,000 with size, Gold and Rainbow in, and the guardrail test counts all three.
- The Garden Isle, part six: weather on the garden (2026-10-24), the plan's sixth piece: Grow a Garden's weather mutations, rebuilt on the game's own day and storms (`data/garden.ts` MUTATIONS). They add as theirs do, a fruit Wet, Moonlit and Shocked selling for 1 + 1 + 1 + 9 = 12 times as much, on top of its size and Gold or Rainbow, never past 100,000 a fruit. Only while the game is played: a fruit that ripened while it was closed gets none of them. Moonlit (twice) is a fruit that ripens at night, one in three; Twilight (three times) one that ripens at dawn or dusk, one in five, like the koi and the ray; worked out from its plant and its count, and only for a fruit that ripened in the last five seconds with the game open (the beds grow every frame it is played, with the time of day). Storms blow only while the boat is out past the buoys, never while it is tied up or ashore, so the garden's storm weather is for a boat out near the isle (within 1,600 of its shore): when a storm breaks, its rain makes each ripe fruit there Wet (twice), one in four ("Rain over the Garden Isle: 3 ripe fruit got Wet"), and the first bolt of lightning strikes one ripe fruit and makes it Shocked, ten times (Grow a Garden's is 100, which at our prices would make every storm the best job in the game), with a bolt down onto its bed and a toast. Sailing round your own isle in a storm is worth it. Each fruit's marks are drawn by it, in a row under its sparkle: blue drops for Wet, a flickering spark for Shocked, a pale blue glow for Moonlit, a pink one for Twilight; picking a marked fruit is a rare catch that says them all ("A Gold, Wet, Moonlit tomato!"), the one worth most of what was picked named. The plan had the lantern gourd always Moonlit at night; it is one in three like the rest, and the dragon fruit fruits every nine minutes, not eight, both so that tended by hand no crop pays more than 2,000 a minute with Moonlit and Twilight in, which the guardrail test now counts.
- The pet system, part one: the pet book (2026-10-24), the first piece of the owner's plan (Roadmap, Pets). The shop's Your pets card (`#petsBtn`, shown once anyone is yours) opens the book (`#petBook`): everyone who is yours, on two shelves. Pets: the dog, the otter, Neil, the crows (one card, with how many of three) and the leggy muffins (one card, how many of six, the owner's call). Companions: the eleven of the roll-call board, in the order they join you. Each card has a picture drawn by the game's own renderer on a little canvas of its own (`render/petsys.ts` portraitView, drawPetPortrait; the companions as they wait at the castle, through drawWaiting with a view), its name, where it came from, and where it is now (the companions: with you, or waiting at the castle). Rename gives any of them but the crows and the muffins a name of your own, up to 16 characters (tidied: no control characters or angle brackets, spaces squeezed, an emoji never cut in half; escaped wherever shown); an empty name, or its old one, gives the old name back, and the card says what it was. The roll-call board uses the new name too. Who is yours is asked as the roll-call board asks it (`HAS_FRIEND`, and `pets.has('dog')`, `otter.free`, `neil.free`, the crows and muffins counts), so nothing about how anyone is won changes and nothing old moves in the save; the names are the one new thing (`pets` in the save, `data/petsys.ts` parsePets, left out while empty). Typing a name never steers the boat: the keys stop at the card. The book, the pure card HTML (`ui/petbook.ts`) and its data are in their own modules.
- The pet system, part two: the drawing pad (2026-10-24), the plan's second piece, the owner's "let kids draw their own pet". The pet book's Pets shelf ends with Draw a pet (the shop's Your pets card now always shows, so a new game can draw before anyone else is yours), which opens the pad (`#petPad`): a square to draw on with a finger (pointer events, captured, `touch-action: none`), six colours (ink, red, yellow, green, blue, pink), Undo, Clear, an ink pot and Done. Each stroke is thinned as it is finished (Ramer-Douglas-Peucker, 0.9 of a cell) and snapped to a 64 by 64 grid, so a point is two characters; a stroke is a colour letter and its points, strokes joined by dots (`data/doodle.ts` encode, decode, Pad). The ink pot is the size cap: 200 points, about 420 characters at most for a whole drawing (a typical one is 100 to 200); when it is dry the pen will not go down and a toast says Undo gives some back. Done asks for a name (up to 16 characters, Doodle if none), with the drawing already walking up and down a strip of sand over it; Keep it puts it on the Pets shelf, after the others, walking on the spot on its card. The walk (`render/petsys.ts` drawDoodle): a flat cut-out on its feet, flipped to face where it goes, bobbing, squashing and stretching a little and leaning in; any separate stroke whose middle is in the bottom third of the drawing and shorter than 60% of its height is a leg, swinging from its top, neighbours in turn (`rig`); one drawn in a single line slides along like a slug. Reduced motion stands it still. Four drawn pets at once (DRAWN_MAX); with four, Draw a pet says to let one go, and Let go on a drawn pet's card asks once more ("You can't get this one back") before it goes to live on the island. Closing the pad keeps the drawing on it for next time. Rename works on drawn pets too; an empty name keeps the one it has. Saved under `pets.drawn` (`{id, nm, d, got}`, `got` the real time it was kept), read back clamped: not a drawing, a twin number or a fifth is dropped, and nothing past the ink pot is read. Walking with you came next (part three).
- The pet system, part three: your walking pet (2026-10-24), the plan's third piece, the owner's call of one walking pet beside the companions. A drawn pet's card in the book has Take it along (Walking with you once it does); taking another along leaves the first in the book, and letting a pet go that walks with you leaves nobody walking. On foot (`entities/petwalk.ts` WalkingPet, added to the scene after the figure) it trots 34 behind the figure along the way it actually walked, a trail point every 3, as the dog and the leggy muffins do, so nothing strands it; more than 60 from its place (a door, the stairs, a portal) it is simply there; it eases to a stand when the figure stops, its legs swinging only as fast as it goes. Aboard it rides the deck at the stern on the side away from the warrior (`petSeat`), a little smaller, bobbing gently with the boat's speed. Drawn by `render/petsys.ts` drawWalking: a shadow, the drawing 34 across its longer side (a little bigger than the dog, so a drawing reads on a phone), facing screen-left or right by its heading, sorted with the figure ashore. A tap on it hops it and sends three hearts up (1.4 s) and a squeak. Saved as `pets.walk` (`d1`), dropped on reading if that pet is not there. Its seat on a dinghy shares the stern with the cat's roof and the crate: the boat is crowded at the smallest hull, as the plan warned.
- The pet system's eggs, the way in (2026-10-24), ahead of part four so the Garden Isle's coop can sell eggs without waiting on the rest: `data/eggs.ts`, pure, nothing a player meets yet. The coop checks `eggRoom(petSave)` (free places in the nest crate, three at most), takes the coins and calls `layEgg(petSave, kind, 'garden')` with a kind of common, rare or mythic; the egg keeps when it was laid and is due as real-time timestamps (10 minutes, an hour, 4 hours, the owner's call), and what will hatch is rolled then and kept hidden (stand-ins: a bunny, frog or ladybird; a hedgehog or fox; a moth), so a reload cannot roll it again. `eggDue` and `eggLeft` work from the clock alone, so an egg hatches with the app closed; a clock gone back counts as no time passed. Saved under `pets.eggs` (`{k, sp, from, at, due}`), read back clamped: an unknown kind dropped, a hatchling its kind cannot hold made its first, a due date no later than its kind's wait, three at most. Part four holds them in the crate, hatches them and plays the reveal.
- The black lagoon's dive and the skull key (2026-10-16), the kid's ("a key in the lagoon: when you dive into the lagoon to fetch the key you see the creatures, ten of them, and your Reapers and things able to go under water go with you"). Ashore on island 11 at the lagoon's edge (within 40 of it) with the scuba gear, the Dive button says Dive into the lagoon (without it, the lagoon's toast says the gear is in island 3's tower), and the diver goes in off the bank: a side-on dive like the trench's and the wreck's (world/lagoondive.ts LAGOON_SITE, 600 across and 300 deep), the first dived from land, so no hull overhead (DiveSite `bank`), and the first with water of its own (DiveSite `look`): murky green under the surface going black long before the bottom, dark round the lamp from the very top (half dark there, 0.88 at the bottom), greenish specks, no sun shafts. Over the surface the jungle leans close, with lily pads; under it the roots trail down along both banks, swaying; the bottom is a bowl of black mud, shallow at the banks and deepest a little right of the middle, with dark weed and pale glowing fungus, and what fell in from the ruins: broken column drums lying in the mud, a standing stump, a block or two, mossy, and old skulls and ribs. On the mud in the deepest part lies the skull key (render/lagoondive.ts): an old key of bone, its bow a grinning skull, glinting pale green over the dark. Swim to it and it is yours (skullKey in the save; 'The skull key! An old key of bone, with a grinning skull for its handle. Somewhere past the portal, a door is waiting for it.'), and the log says Skull key found. Ten lagoon creatures live there (entities/lagoonfolk.ts), the Old One's kind from Gigantis seen swimming side on: a long scaly green body, a fin crest, frog legs kicking behind, webbed claws ahead, and a big pale eye that glows over the dark, so one beyond the lamp shows as an eye watching. Each keeps to its own place, two near the top, more in the middle water and three round the key, so they come a few at a time. One notices a diver within 120 and swims at it at 72, slower than a diver swims (115), so a diver can always get away; within 24 it raises its claws for 0.6 s (the warning), and if the diver is still within 38 it claws the tank for 7 seconds of air, with bubbles, then backs off 1.6 s and comes again; past 240 or with the diver on the way up, it goes home. Two hit points each, as on land: a spear hits for its power (the barbed spear beats one in a throw), and beaten, one gives up and swims off down into the dark for the rest of the dive ('2 left down here'); they are all back the next dive. The aim ring picks the nearest one ahead. And the friends who can go under water go down too, if they are with you ashore: the naga, whole, his tail streaming out behind him, keeping up behind the diver, and the baby Reaper, legs paddling, down behind; each goes for any creature within 150 of the diver, the naga's trident for 2 every 1.3 s and the baby's nip for 1 every second, as ashore. Out of air, the diver floats back up to the bank; back up, the figure is on the bank where it went in. The portal's door is what the key is for (below).
- Island 11's old portal, the skull door and the crypt (2026-10-16), the kid's ("a portal to the Old One's area; after fetching the skull key, open the door behind, in the Old One's room: skeletons 5, zombies 6, ghosts 4, and the Forgotten One again, he fooled you as he laughs"; "the Forgotten One: 9 hp and 4 damage, only him; when defeated he is still alive"). The portal on island 11's ruins wakes once the Old One is beaten (isle7Stage 4): its dark inside turns a swirling green, bright at its heart (render/isle11.ts), and its toast says it goes to the Old One's place; before that it is dark, and the toast says something old is still awake on the other side, the Old One on Gigantis. Walk to just in front of it (world/isle11.ts PORTAL11_FRONT, within 28) and Into the portal takes the figure into the ruins behind the throne on Gigantis, as a tower of its own (main.js OLDPLACE: in at the ruins, home to island 11 in front of the portal, Back through the portal the way out; the castle's own rules for its rooms hold for both ways in). At the back of the ruins, in the wall behind the black pool, is a new door (world/gigantis.ts SKULL_DOOR, drawn by render/gigantis.ts drawSkullDoor): the castle's stone arch with a skull carved over it, its wooden door with a keyhole shaped like a skull in bone. Without the key, close to it, a toast says what it is and that its key lies at the bottom of a black lagoon, far away; with the skull key (and the Old One beaten) it stands open, glowing green, and Open the skull door goes through, hearts filled, into the crypt (world/tower.ts CRYPT, room 24, drawn as the castle's halls): five skeletons in front, six zombies behind them and four ghosts at the back, in arcs round the back of the room (the kid's numbers, read as how many, not hit points: they are the castle's own skeletons, zombies and ghosts), and the Forgotten One again (entities/forgotten.ts, a second one with 9 hit points; his blows, his skulls and his ray all hit for 4 hearts). They wake as the figure comes in: 'The Forgotten One! On his feet again, laughing: "Ha ha ha! You thought you beat me? I fooled you!"'. Brought to nothing he does not fall apart: he yields (a new state), sinking to his knees over 0.9 s, his cloak spread round him and his sword point down, still alive ('Enough!'). With him yielded and the crypt clear, whichever is last, it is won (cryptWon in the save): hearts filled, 6,000 coins, 'You beat the Forgotten One again! He kneels, still alive. "You beat me," he says. "Fair and square. I have something to show you..."', and the log says The Forgotten One again. After, the crypt is quiet and he kneels where he was beaten, waiting for you to go to him (his ending, below).
- The Forgotten One's ending (2026-10-16), the kid's ("he shows you the castle: both [walks round it with you, and a cutscene], and now it's your headquarters, and he saves you from the Old One, exploding the Old One into black lagoon water"; and the portable scanner he gives you). Up close to him kneeling in the crypt, Go with him starts it, once (castleHQ in the save; a game that won the crypt before this sees it the next time it goes in). It is a cutscene: the bars come down, its words are in the lower one, the buttons go, and the figure walks with him by itself, a step behind and to his side (main.js fgFollow, the stick's own vector toward that spot), while he walks to show each room. He has a new state for it (entities/forgotten.ts guide): up off his knees, sword point down at his side, not screaming, not to be fought, walking where the game sends him at a little over his fight pace (GUIDE). The scene, about 38 seconds (main.js fgSteps): the crypt ('"Come," says the Forgotten One, getting up. "I will show you my castle."'), his throne room ('"My throne. I sat on it so long that the world forgot me."'), a hall, and the courtyard, where he turns to you ('"It is all yours now. Gigantis is your headquarters!"') and your flag runs up a new pole there, and he gives you his portable scanner ('"Point it at a creature, and it tells you its secrets."'). Then back through the ruins: the black pool boils, the Old One rises ('He wants you dead!') and roars, and the Forgotten One runs at him ('"Not this one!"') and bursts into bones, his skull among them, in a white flash, and the Old One is blown apart into a fountain of black water (bits of both fall and fade). 'He gave everything to save you. The Old One is gone for good.' At the end the save takes Gigantis as your headquarters and the scanner (scanner 1), and the log says Gigantis, your headquarters and Portable scanner. After: the castle has nobody in it to fight (every room clear, its doors open, the throne room empty), your flag flies over the courtyard, the crypt is empty, and the Old One never rises again, not even for a tester's skipped scene. What the headquarters does, and what the scanner tells you, are the kid's still (asked); the scanner does nothing yet.
- The portable scanner (2026-10-16), the kid's ("yes, and tells weakness of it, like Star: he is afraid of bigger stuff, or the Reaper: afraid of honey"), the Forgotten One's gift. With it (scanner 1 or more), a blue scan button sits under the throw button whenever there is something to scan: ashore, what the spear would aim at (main.js scanKindOf names its kind by where it lives: a horde's unit by its kind, the lancers, gorillas, gators, monkeys and hired men by their camps, the bosses by who they are), and aboard, the nearest beast the harpoon would see, within 260 of the boat (`data/scans.ts` SCAN_SEA). A tap sends a dashed blue beam out to it with rings spreading round it for 0.9 s, then its blue screen shows where the toasts go (they wait behind it) for 11 s or until tapped: its name, How to beat it (what the game does: the warning to watch for, when it can be hit, with what, in a sentence or two, each checked against the code) and, where it has one, its Weakness (Star's and both Reapers' are the kid's: afraid of bigger stuff, afraid of honey, ahead of the game). Thirty-five kinds: the nine beasts at sea and twenty-six on foot. Each kind scanned is kept (the save's scanned), the first time with New!, and the log's Portable scanner chip counts them. The swamp's creatures, which nobody fights, are not scanned yet.
- The scanner gets better (2026-10-16), the kid's (asked what a better scanner does: further, eggs, hit points? "all").
